Outside of work, I’ve skipped several dance nights and mentoring sessions because I just felt too overwhelmed to show up.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have any intrusive thoughts that are unrelated to your worries about work or everyday responsibilities?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / computer_support_specialist / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Outside of work, I’ve skipped several dance nights and mentoring sessions because I just felt too overwhelmed to show up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any intrusive thoughts that are unrelated to your worries about work or everyday responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get stuck on worries, but they’re usually tied to something real I’m responsible for. I haven’t noticed random intrusive thoughts outside of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes and included symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve had anything that suddenly peaks like that within a few minutes. I get anxious and uncomfortable, but I haven’t really noticed it coming on as a sudden attack with those specific symptoms.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge, like you can't relax or settle down?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel wound up a lot of the time, like my body is bracing for something to go wrong. Even when I’m just sitting at my desk or trying to relax at dance night, it’s hard to really settle down.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about a specific thing or are they more about just a general feeling of uncertainty or unease?\n\nPatient: It’s more than one specific thing. It used to be more focused on the condo, but now it kind of jumps around — like whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll mess up on a support call, or how I’m ever going to save money and move forward. It’s like there’s always something for my mind to grab onto.\n\nDoctor: Is there one area of your life where your worries seem to be most prominent?\n\nPatient: Not really one area anymore. It used to be mostly about the condo, but now it jumps around a lot — like whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll make a mistake on a support call, or how I’m ever going to save money and move forward. It just sort of shifts from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore or suppress them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. Even when I tell myself, like, “I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I already sent that email,” the thought keeps coming back. Then it turns into more what-ifs, and before I know it I’m thinking through five different things that could go wrong. It takes me a while to pull myself out of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s like my body is always bracing for the next bad thing, even when I’m just sitting at my desk or trying to relax and enjoy dance night.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot of the time it’s work-related stuff, like I’ll tell myself I already checked the spreadsheet or sent the email, but the thought keeps coming back. Then it turns into, “What if I missed something?” and then five other “what ifs” before I can get myself to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain things over and over, like checking or cleaning, to try and get rid of these worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that kind of way. I might look back over an email or a spreadsheet, but it’s more because I’m worried I made a mistake, not because I feel like I have to do a ritual or keep doing it until something feels “right.”\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I get anxious and uncomfortable, but I haven’t really noticed it coming on as a sudden attack with those specific symptoms.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge, like you can't relax or settle down?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel wound up a lot of the time, like my body is bracing for something to go wrong. Even when I’m just sitting at my desk or trying to relax at dance night, it’s hard to really settle down.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about a specific thing or are they more about just a general feeling of uncertainty or unease?\n\nPatient: It’s more than one specific thing. It’s like there’s always something for my mind to grab onto.\n\nDoctor: Is there one area of your life where your worries seem to be most prominent?\n\nPatient: Not really one area anymore. It used to be mostly about the condo, but now it jumps around a lot — like whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll make a mistake on a support call, or how I’m ever going to save money and move forward. It takes me a while to pull myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are intrusive, unwanted, and unrelated to your worries about work or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. So there aren’t many times where I feel completely at ease.

Then it turns into, “What if I missed something?” and then five other “what ifs” before I can get myself to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain things over and over, like checking or cleaning, to try and get rid of these worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that kind of way. I might look back over an email or a spreadsheet, but it’s more because I’m worried I made a mistake, not because I feel like I have to do a ritual or keep doing it until something feels “right.”\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. At work I’ve been falling behind on support tickets because I keep double-checking even simple things, and coworkers have had to step in sometimes. Outside of work, I’ve skipped several dance nights and mentoring sessions because I just felt too overwhelmed to show up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any intrusive thoughts that are unrelated to your worries about work or everyday responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get stuck on worries, but they’re usually tied to something real I’m responsible for. I haven’t noticed random intrusive thoughts outside of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes and included symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve had anything that suddenly peaks like that within a few minutes. I get anxious and uncomfortable, but I haven’t really noticed it coming on as a sudden attack with those specific symptoms.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge, like you can't relax or settle down?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel wound up a lot of the time, like my body is bracing for something to go wrong. Even when I’m just sitting at my desk or trying to relax at dance night, it’s hard to really settle down.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about a specific thing or are they more about just a general feeling of uncertainty or unease?\n\nPatient: It’s more than one specific thing. It used to be more focused on the condo, but now it kind of jumps around — like whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll mess up on a support call, or how I’m ever going to save money and move forward. It’s like there’s always something for my mind to grab onto.\n\nDoctor: Is there one area of your life where your worries seem to be most prominent?\n\nPatient: Not really one area anymore. It used to be mostly about the condo, but now it jumps around a lot — like whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll make a mistake on a support call, or how I’m ever going to save money and move forward. It just sort of shifts from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore or suppress them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. Even when I tell myself, like, “I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I already sent that email,” the thought keeps coming back. Then it turns into more what-ifs, and before I know it I’m thinking through five different things that could go wrong. It takes me a while to pull myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are intrusive, unwanted, and unrelated to your worries about work or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. The thoughts that bother me are usually tied to something I’m worried about, like work or money or whether I did something wrong. I don’t really notice random intrusive thoughts that are separate from that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are persistent, unwanted, and intrusive, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s usually the same worry looping even after I’ve tried to tell myself it’s handled. Like I know I already checked the spreadsheet or sent the email, but the thought comes back anyway, and then one “what if” turns into a bunch more before I can calm it down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you feel completely at ease and your worries seem to fade away?\n\nPatient: Not very often. Even if one worry settles down, it kind of shifts to something else. Like it won’t just stay on the condo issue—it jumps to whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll mess up on a support call, or how I’m supposed to save money and move forward. So there aren’t many times where I feel completely at ease.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships, or are they more about just a general feeling of anxiety without a specific focus?\n\nPatient: It’s usually about everyday things, but it moves around a lot. It’s not just the condo anymore. I’ll start worrying whether my cousin is upset with me, then whether I’m going to make a mistake on a support call, then how I’m ever going to save money or move forward with my life. It doesn’t really stay in one place.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’ve been feeling really wound up almost all the time. It’s like my body is always bracing for the next bad thing, even when I’m just sitting at my desk or trying to relax and enjoy dance night.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot of the time it’s work-related stuff, like I’ll tell myself I already checked the spreadsheet or sent the email, but the thought keeps coming back. Then it turns into, “What if I missed something?” and then five other “what ifs” before I can get myself to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain things over and over, like checking or cleaning, to try and get rid of these worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that kind of way. I might look back over an email or a spreadsheet, but it’s more because I’m worried I made a mistake, not because I feel like I have to do a ritual or keep doing it until something feels “right.”\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. At work I’ve been falling behind on support tickets because I keep double-checking even simple things, and coworkers have had to step in sometimes. Outside of work, I’ve skipped several dance nights and mentoring sessions because I just felt too overwhelmed to show up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any intrusive thoughts that are unrelated to your worries about work or everyday responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get stuck on worries, but they’re usually tied to something real I’m responsible for. I haven’t noticed random intrusive thoughts outside of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes and included symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve had anything that suddenly peaks like that within a few minutes. I get anxious and uncomfortable, but I haven’t really noticed it coming on as a sudden attack with those specific symptoms.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge, like you can't relax or settle down?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel wound up a lot of the time, like my body is bracing for something to go wrong. Even when I’m just sitting at my desk or trying to relax at dance night, it’s hard to really settle down.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about a specific thing or are they more about just a general feeling of uncertainty or unease?\n\nPatient: It’s more than one specific thing. It used to be more focused on the condo, but now it kind of jumps around — like whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll mess up on a support call, or how I’m ever going to save money and move forward. It’s like there’s always something for my mind to grab onto.\n\nDoctor: Is there one area of your life where your worries seem to be most prominent?\n\nPatient: Not really one area anymore. It used to be mostly about the condo, but now it jumps around a lot — like whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll make a mistake on a support call, or how I’m ever going to save money and move forward. It just sort of shifts from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore or suppress them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. Even when I tell myself, like, “I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I already sent that email,” the thought keeps coming back. Then it turns into more what-ifs, and before I know it I’m thinking through five different things that could go wrong. It takes me a while to pull myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are intrusive, unwanted, and unrelated to your worries about work or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. The thoughts that bother me are usually tied to something I’m worried about, like work or money or whether I did something wrong. I don’t really notice random intrusive thoughts that are separate from that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are persistent, unwanted, and intrusive, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s usually the same worry looping even after I’ve tried to tell myself it’s handled. Like I know I already checked the spreadsheet or sent the email, but the thought comes back anyway, and then one “what if” turns into a bunch more before I can calm it down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you feel completely at ease and your worries seem to fade away?\n\nPatient: Not very often. Even if one worry settles down, it kind of shifts to something else. Like it won’t just stay on the condo issue—it jumps to whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll mess up on a support call, or how I’m supposed to save money and move forward. So there aren’t many times where I feel completely at ease.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships, or are they more about just a general feeling of anxiety without a specific focus?\n\nPatient: It’s usually about everyday things, but it moves around a lot. It’s not just the condo anymore. I’ll start worrying whether my cousin is upset with me, then whether I’m going to make a mistake on a support call, then how I’m ever going to save money or move forward with my life. It doesn’t really stay in one place.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you never worry about, any area of your life where you consistently feel confident and worry-free?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I can’t think of an area that feels totally worry-free. It doesn’t really stay in one place anymore. I’ll be worrying about the condo, and then it jumps to whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll mess up a support call, or how I’m ever going to save money and move forward. So… no, not consistently.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries could be described as excessive and uncontrollable, even when you try to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I tell myself, “I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I already sent that email,” it doesn’t really stop. The thought just keeps looping, and then one “what if” turns into several more before I can pull myself back.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’ve been feeling really wound up almost all the time. It’s like my body is always bracing for the next bad thing, even when I’m just sitting at my desk or trying to relax and enjoy dance night.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot of the time it’s work-related stuff, like I’ll tell myself I already checked the spreadsheet or sent the email, but the thought keeps coming back. Then it turns into, “What if I missed something?” and then five other “what ifs” before I can get myself to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain things over and over, like checking or cleaning, to try and get rid of these worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that kind of way. I might look back over an email or a spreadsheet, but it’s more because I’m worried I made a mistake, not because I feel like I have to do a ritual or keep doing it until something feels “right.”\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. At work I’ve been falling behind on support tickets because I keep double-checking even simple things, and coworkers have had to step in sometimes. Outside of work, I’ve skipped several dance nights and mentoring sessions because I just felt too overwhelmed to show up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any intrusive thoughts that are unrelated to your worries about work or everyday responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I get stuck on worries, but they’re usually tied to something real I’m responsible for. I haven’t noticed random intrusive thoughts outside of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes and included symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve had anything that suddenly peaks like that within a few minutes. I get anxious and uncomfortable, but I haven’t really noticed it coming on as a sudden attack with those specific symptoms.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge, like you can't relax or settle down?\n\nPatient: Yes. I feel wound up a lot of the time, like my body is bracing for something to go wrong. Even when I’m just sitting at my desk or trying to relax at dance night, it’s hard to really settle down.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about a specific thing or are they more about just a general feeling of uncertainty or unease?\n\nPatient: It’s more than one specific thing. It used to be more focused on the condo, but now it kind of jumps around — like whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll mess up on a support call, or how I’m ever going to save money and move forward. It’s like there’s always something for my mind to grab onto.\n\nDoctor: Is there one area of your life where your worries seem to be most prominent?\n\nPatient: Not really one area anymore. It used to be mostly about the condo, but now it jumps around a lot — like whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll make a mistake on a support call, or how I’m ever going to save money and move forward. It just sort of shifts from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore or suppress them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. Even when I tell myself, like, “I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I already sent that email,” the thought keeps coming back. Then it turns into more what-ifs, and before I know it I’m thinking through five different things that could go wrong. It takes me a while to pull myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are intrusive, unwanted, and unrelated to your worries about work or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. The thoughts that bother me are usually tied to something I’m worried about, like work or money or whether I did something wrong. I don’t really notice random intrusive thoughts that are separate from that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are persistent, unwanted, and intrusive, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s usually the same worry looping even after I’ve tried to tell myself it’s handled. Like I know I already checked the spreadsheet or sent the email, but the thought comes back anyway, and then one “what if” turns into a bunch more before I can calm it down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you feel completely at ease and your worries seem to fade away?\n\nPatient: Not very often. Even if one worry settles down, it kind of shifts to something else. It’s not just the condo anymore. I’ll start worrying whether my cousin is upset with me, then whether I’m going to make a mistake on a support call, then how I’m ever going to save money or move forward with my life. It doesn’t really stay in one place.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you never worry about, any area of your life where you consistently feel confident and worry-free?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I can’t think of an area that feels totally worry-free. It doesn’t really stay in one place anymore. I’ll be worrying about the condo, and then it jumps to whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll mess up a support call, or how I’m ever going to save money and move forward. So… no, not consistently.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries could be described as excessive and uncontrollable, even when you try to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I tell myself, “I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I already sent that email,” it doesn’t really stop. It used to be more focused on the condo, but now it kind of jumps around — like whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll mess up on a support call, or how I’m ever going to save money and move forward. It used to be mostly about the condo, but now it jumps around a lot — like whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll make a mistake on a support call, or how I’m ever going to save money and move forward. It just sort of shifts from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore or suppress them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. Even when I tell myself, like, “I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I already sent that email,” the thought keeps coming back. Then it turns into more what-ifs, and before I know it I’m thinking through five different things that could go wrong. It takes me a while to pull myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are intrusive, unwanted, and unrelated to your worries about work or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. The thoughts that bother me are usually tied to something I’m worried about, like work or money or whether I did something wrong. I don’t really notice random intrusive thoughts that are separate from that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are persistent, unwanted, and intrusive, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s usually the same worry looping even after I’ve tried to tell myself it’s handled. Like I know I already checked the spreadsheet or sent the email, but the thought comes back anyway, and then one “what if” turns into a bunch more before I can calm it down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you feel completely at ease and your worries seem to fade away?\n\nPatient: Not very often. Even if one worry settles down, it kind of shifts to something else. Like it won’t just stay on the condo issue—it jumps to whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll mess up on a support call, or how I’m supposed to save money and move forward. So there aren’t many times where I feel completely at ease.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships, or are they more about just a general feeling of anxiety without a specific focus?\n\nPatient: It’s usually about everyday things, but it moves around a lot. It’s not just the condo anymore. I’ll start worrying whether my cousin is upset with me, then whether I’m going to make a mistake on a support call, then how I’m ever going to save money or move forward with my life. It doesn’t really stay in one place.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything that you never worry about, any area of your life where you consistently feel confident and worry-free?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I can’t think of an area that feels totally worry-free. It doesn’t really stay in one place anymore. I’ll be worrying about the condo, and then it jumps to whether my cousin is upset with me, or whether I’ll mess up a support call, or how I’m ever going to save money and move forward. So… no, not consistently.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries could be described as excessive and uncontrollable, even when you try to focus on other things?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I tell myself, “I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I already sent that email,” it doesn’t really stop. The thought just keeps looping, and then one “what if” turns into several more before I can pull myself back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are usually about things that could realistically happen?\n\nPatient: Some of them could happen, I guess. Like I could make a mistake on a support call, or my cousin could be upset with me, or I could struggle to save money. But my mind takes those things and runs with them. It doesn’t just stay on one concern, like the condo—it jumps from one realistic “what if” to another until it feels bigger than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have your worries been causing problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes. At work I’ve started falling behind on support tickets because I keep double-checking even simple things, and then coworkers have had to help cover what I’m not getting done. And outside of work, I’ve skipped a few dance nights and mentoring sessions because I just felt too overwhelmed to show up. So it is affecting my day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are intrusive, unwanted, and cause you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. They feel unwanted because I’m not trying to think about them, but they just keep coming back. Like I’ll tell myself I already checked the spreadsheet or already sent the email, and it still loops in my head. Then one “what if” turns into five more, and it gets really upsetting before I can pull myself out of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
